The attacker used credentials for an AWS account that Qubole's platform uses to reach those customers. How the attacker got those credentials has not been disclosed.   Qubole , a data lake platform owned by Idera, operates within its customers' AWS accounts. To let it in, a customer creates an IAM role, which is an AWS identity with its own set of permissions.   The role's trust policy allows Qubole's AWS account to assume the role, granting those permissions. Each request to do this, called AssumeRole , must include a matching code from Qubole known as an External ID.   Apart from the attack, most of the roles the attacker assumed were unused, according to Wiz's researchers. But each role still trusted Qubole's account, so the attacker's requests worked.   Any organization...</a><br>

Both releases download and run code built to steal the wallet key that Swarm Desktop keeps for its node, along with the stored password that unlocks it. The code is also built to install a backdoor on Windows, macOS, and Linux machines.   The bee-js package is the JavaScript library behind Swarm Desktop and the swarm-cli command-line tool. Swarm Desktop runs a Swarm node on a user's own computer and saves the node's private keys there.   Both malicious releases have been taken down, and the project's maintainers released a clean Swarm Desktop update today. Both apps signed customers in through ForgeRock , an identity platform now part of Ping Identity , and on those servers the researchers reached an administrator console that still used its default password.   They tested the full attack only on their own and consenting owners' cars in the United States, in February 2026, and both systems were fixed about two hours after they reported the problem.   Shubham Shah and Sam Curry published their work late last week. They had started with the apps: both brands signed customers in through the same platform, which runs in two parts: one for login and one, called OpenIDM , that holds customer records. Keplr uses a company called Web3Auth to run that Google and Apple sign-in. The attacker used a security flaw at that company to reach the wallets, both companies said late last week.   Web3Auth then used the attacker's own route to empty other at-risk wallets before the attacker got to them, it said. It moved that money, with help from the wallet maker, into recovery wallets for safekeeping. So an empty balance may mean the money was rescued, not stolen.   Wallets made with a recovery phrase (a backup list of 12 or 24 words) or a hardware wallet are not affected, the wallet maker said. It has paused Google and Apple sign-in while it investigates. When a user selects " Yes, and don't ask again ," Anthropic's AI coding assistant can save the command, word-for-word, including the secret, in a project settings file.   Software security company Apiiro said yesterday that 1,122 public copies of that file hold at least one secret, though it did not test whether any still work. One company the researchers notified earlier this week confirmed its key had been live.   The file is .claude/settings.local.json. Removing it from a project does not undo the leak, because older versions stay in git history and in forks of the repository. The researchers' advice is to rotate first: change every password, token, and key the file has ever held, then clean up.   How a Secret Lands in the File   By default, Claude Code asks before it runs most shell commands. If the u...</a><br>

The update that installs the fix still unpacks its files using the site's old, unpatched code, so an attacker who strikes at the right moment can run code on the server. The researcher advises blocking web access to the site's wp-content/upgrade folder before updating.   The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-119977 , affects WordPress 4.7 through 7.1.3. During a core update that uses a complete package, WordPress unpacks a copy of the new version into a temporary folder inside wp-content/upgrade. That copy includes the WordPress installer, and the web server can usually reach it.   An attacker with no account who can determine the folder's name and access the installer before WordPress deletes the copy can end up running code on the server. The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) ad...</a><br>

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-123158  (CVSS score: 8.1) and codenamed SSHole (or *sshole), impacts Linux systems that run OpenSSH on the musl C library with Pluggable Authentication Modules (PAM) disabled, including Alpine Linux's default OpenSSH server configuration.   An unauthenticated attacker  can supply an oversized password to access accounts that are deliberately configured without usable passwords, without having to steal credentials or SSH keys. The issue, discovered  by security researcher Ozgun Kultekin, has been addressed in OpenSSH 10.6p2 .   The underlying issue dates back to a patch introduced in 2016 to fix a username enumeration vulnerability tracked as CVE-2016-6210 .
